Search for Leptoquark in the𝝉𝝂𝒃 Final State2
The ATLAS Collaboration1, Jiaqi Zanga, Takuya Nobea, Junichi Tanakaa,3
Tomoya Iizawaa, Stergios Kazakosb

aICEPP, The University of Tokyo,bMichigan State University5
This note reports the first search for leptoquark signals in the𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state with the ATLAS
experiment, motivated by the𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) anomaly, using the Run-2 dataset with 140 fb−1 of an
integrated luminosity at√𝑠 = 13TeV.

1 Executive Summary188
1.1 Target189
This is a search for leptoquark signals in the𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state, motivated by the𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) anomalies by the190
BaBar, Belle and LHCb experiments. It is a completely new phase space, previously not explored by191
ATLAS and CMS1. Since the signal strength scales with the 4th power of the coupling constant, the192
sensitivity gain from increased integrated luminosity is rather limited (of course the limit on the signal193
cross-section will be improved by adding more data, though). We agreed with PC/PubCom and relevant194
conveners to use the Run-2 dataset only and aim for an early publication by the end of 2025.195
1.2 Context and Motivation196
Motivate this analysis in 1 paragraph: why is this signature interesting? Which kind of197
models are you probing?198
While the 𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) anomaly requires both𝑏𝜏 and 𝑐𝜈 couplings of the leptoquark, previous ATLAS199
and CMS searches, focusing on the𝜏𝜏 final state, were sensitive only to the𝑏𝜏 coupling and typically200
fixed the𝑐𝜈 coupling to a small value. This assumption was motivated not by experimental constraints201
but by theoretical considerations: a general UV completion to suppressΔ𝐹 = 2 transitions. A more202
model-independent approach is crucial, as the UV model may include mechanisms that naturally suppress203
flavor transitions, such as the GIM mechanism in the SM. Our search in the𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state is sensitive to204
both 𝑏𝜏 and 𝑐𝜈 couplings simultaneously, and thus uniquely probes scenarios with larger𝑐𝜈 couplings that205
were previously unexplored.206
How is the analysis done in 1 paragraph: what are the main BG processes and how do you207
estimate them (are they MC- or data-driven, what is the general idea of the control regions,208
...), general characteristics of the PL fit (which distribution, binned?, ...)209
We will perform a simple cut-and-count analysis with two single-bin SRs. Events with high-𝑝T hadronic 𝜏210
and high-𝐸miss
T are selected. Two SRs are categorized by the𝑏-jet 𝑝T: one with a high-𝑝T 𝑏-jet for an211
on-shell leptoquark signal decaying to𝑏𝜏; and the other with low-𝑝T 𝑏-jet for a non-resonant𝜏𝜈 signal via212
𝑡-channel exchange of the leptoquark. The main SM background is(𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈)+jets. Top-quark production213
(𝑡¯𝑡 and single-𝑡) is subdominant. For these components, dedicated CRs are prepared and involved in214
1 There was a𝑊+𝑏 measurements in the past, but it employed𝑒 and 𝜇 channels only [1]. There were searches for SUSY or
Leptoquark signals in𝑏, 𝜏 and 𝐸miss
T final states, but they required multiple𝑏 or 𝜏 [2–4], but we couldn’t find searches in
ATLAS with a single𝜏, single𝑏-jet and large𝐸miss
T .

ATLAS DRAFT
the likelihood to give a constraint from data to the MC simulation. WCR is defined by replacing the215
𝜏 in the SR with an electron or muon. TopCR requires an additional electron or muon to the SR. The216
background estimation strategy is checked carefully in the VRs. The SM background with fake𝜏 lepton217
(mostly from (𝑍 → 𝜈𝜈)+jets) is expected to have very small yields, and will be estimated by the MC218
simulationdirectly, afteracarefulcheckin (𝑍 → ℓℓ)+fake-𝜏 VR.Forus, thefake 𝜏 rejectionisnotamajor219
concern, and the sensitivity depends mostly on the signal efficiency. We will therefore use the RNNTau220
identification, without waiting for the latest recommendation for the GNTau, for which we already have a221
CP recommendation for the Run-2 dataset.222

the further steps to be taken as of the time of writing. Details are not provided here but in the dedicated226

2 Introduction231
A test of the lepton flavor universality (LFU) has been made via the ratio of the branching fractions232
𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) = B (𝐵 → 𝐷 (∗) 𝜏𝜈)/B (𝐵 → 𝐷 (∗) ℓ𝜈) by the BaBar[5, 6], Belle[7–10], Belle-II[11, 12] and233
LHCb[13–16] experiments, whereℓ = 𝑒, 𝜇 (ℓ = 𝜇 for LHCb). The latest average of these measurements234
calculated by the HFLAV collaboration[17] is shown in Figure 2.1. It reports𝑅(𝐷) and 𝑅(𝐷∗) exceed the235
Standard Model (SM) predictions[18–27] by2.5𝜎 and 2.3𝜎 respectively. Considering the𝑅(𝐷)-𝑅(𝐷∗)236
correlation of−0.374, the resulting combined𝜒2 is 17.63 for 2 degree of freedom, corresponding to a237
𝑝-value of1.48 × 10−4. The difference with the SM predictions corresponds to about3.8𝜎. This can be a238
hint of a new physics beyond the SM, and it is important for Belle-II and LHCb to follow it up with more239
data.240

Figure 2.1: Average of𝑅(𝐷∗) and 𝑅(𝐷)[17] measured by the BaBar[5, 6], Belle[7–10], Belle-II[11, 12], and
LHCb[14–16] experiments.
Apossiblesolutiontoexplainthe 𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) anomalyistointroduceahypotheticalnewparticle,aleptoquark,241
which couples to both leptons and quarks, as shown in Fig. 2.2. A comprehensive program to search for242
leptoquarksignalswith 𝜏leptonsinthefinalstatehasbeenconductedbytheATLASandCMSexperiments,243
forbothscalarandvectorleptoquarkscenarios[28–31]. Assumingtheleptoquarkpairproduction,thecross244
section depends solely on the strong coupling constant at the leading order, making the searches mostly245
model-independent. Signals with leptoquark masses below approximately 1.5–1.8TeVwere excluded by246
such searches. For the single leptoquark production, searches in the𝑏𝜏𝜏 final state were performed. In the247
case of the large coupling constant,𝑡-channel exchange of the leptoquark is expected to lead to an excess of248
eventsinthehigh-massregionofSMprocesses. ATLASandCMSperformedsucha“non-resonantsearch”249
for the leptoquark in the𝜏𝜏 final state. ATLAS excluded the region approximately𝜆𝑏𝜏 /𝑀LQ > 1TeV−1,250
where 𝜆𝑏𝜏 and 𝑀LQ are the coupling constant and mass of the leptoquark, defined in Section 3. For the251
vector leptoquark models, the result partially tested a parameter region consistent with𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) anomaly.252

CMS had a similar sensitivity, but observed a small excess of events at the high𝑚 𝜏 𝜏, leading to a weaker253

All of the results above are summarized in Figures 2.4 and 2.5 for ATLAS and CMS, respectively. For255
these analyses, signal strength was assumed to depend only on𝑏𝜏 coupling of the leptoquark, but the256
𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) anomaly requires both𝑏𝜏 and 𝑐𝜈 couplings.257
This note reports the first search for the leptoquark signals in the𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state with the ATLAS detector,258
which is sensitive to the product of both couplings. As shown in Figure 2.3, our target signal is exactly259
the same structure as the𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) diagram (Fig. 2.2 (b)). The presence of a𝑏-jet mitigates the effect of260
interference with the SM processes, and suppress the background in SRs strongly. This phase space has261
never been explored by ATLAS/CMS. CMS had a leptoquark interpretation in the𝜏𝜈 final state in their262
𝑊 ′ → 𝜏𝜈 paper[32], but the sensitivity was not so high since it did not require any𝑏-jets.263
For the strong physics motivation described in Section 3, it has been justified to use the Run-2 dataset only264
of an integrated luminosity 140fb−1, and we aim to publish the results by the end of 2025. We agreed with265
PC/PubComm/EXOT and LPX conveners about the publication strategy. Some more detailed discussion266
about the physics motivation is found in Section 3.267

In this section, some details of the Monte-Carlo simulation used in this analysis are discussed.333

of the Universal FeynRules Output (UFO) model files is found in Ref. [39]. The full Lagrangian related to338

The parameter𝜅 introduces the LQ couplings to the SM gauge-boson fields. The model with𝜅 = 1is the345
minimal 𝑈1 model, and the one with𝜅 = 0is called as the Yang-Mills𝑈1 model.346
OurMCsampleis 𝜏𝜈 𝜏 withup-to2jets,where“jet”sherearegluon, 𝑢, 𝑑, 𝑐, 𝑠,or 𝑏quark. WeuseMC20a/d/e347
configurations at√𝑠 = 13 TeV. Diagrams with 2 new physics couplings (NP == 2) are categorized as348
“BSM”, while those withNP2 == 2 are categorized as “interference” with the SM𝜏𝜈 processes. The349
MadGraph can identify BSM and interference event-by-event. For the initial test (Section A.1), BSM,350
interference, and BSM+internfernce without SM (NP2 > 0) events are generated separately at each mass.351
They are calledbsm, inf, andbi, respectively. These words can be found in the dataset name of our352
signal samples. Since the 𝑈1 leptoquark is exchanged in both𝑠-channel (resonance) and 𝑡-channel353
(non-resonant) topologies, and the full matrix element (ME) is simulated coherently, these components354
cannot be disentangled in the generation step. Signal samples include not only the target diagrams of355
this analysis in Fig. 2.3, but also for example a pair production of LQs decaying into𝑈1𝑈1 → 𝑏𝜏𝑐𝜈,356
depending on the LQ mass. The NNPDF3.0NLO (LHAPDF ID= 260800) and the five-flavor scheme are357
used forMadGraph5_aMC@NLO and the NNPDF2.3LO is used forPythia8. For the CKKW-L matching,358
the 𝑘𝑡 𝐷𝑢𝑟 ℎ𝑎𝑚 and 𝑑 parameters are set to 15GeVand 0.4, respectively. It means that jets originated from359
theMEareproducedwith 𝑝T >15GeV. TheAF3ATLASdetectorsimulationisused(a907). The Geant4360

combinedwiththesignalsample. ToinvestigatethesignalcontaminationintheCR/VRdefinedinSection7,368
the inclusive tau-decay is adopted instead of only the hadronic decay. Validation plots are available in369
ATLMCPROD-12046, ATLMCPROD-10977 andhttps://indico.cern.ch/event/1353419/.370

5 Analysis Framework and Object Definitions386
Object definitions and event pre-selections are summarized in this section.387
5.1 Tau+X Framework388
The analysis is based on the Tau+X framework developed under the Exotics Tau+X umbrella efforts.389
The DAOD_PHYS derivation is used with p6479 (data) and p6490 (MC). Events are first preprocessed390
by TopCPTookKit 2.17.1 for the calibrations by CP tools, selections of physics objects and event391
pre-selections. AnalysisBase release is 25.2.51. The latest R22 recommended electron efficiency SF392
setup is done manually through the official map file with the local checkout (ElectronEfficiencyCorrec-393
tion/2015_2025/rel22.2/2025_Run2Rel22_Recommendation_v3/map0.txt). The modification can be found394
on GitLab.395
The selected events by TopCPToolKit are further processed by the FastFrame framework. At this stage,396
some more detailed event selections are applied for SR/CR/VR and histograms/light-weight trees are saved,397
that can be used as an input for the TRexFitter statistical framework directly.398
5.2 Electron Selections399
The “baseline” electron selections to be used for the overlap removal and𝐸miss
T reconstruction are400
summarized in Table 5.1. For the “signal” electrons used in the SR/CR/VR, we requireHighPtCaloOnly401
isolation on top of that, to suppress the high-𝑝T fake electrons.402

5.4 Hadronic Tau Selections416
The “baseline” tau selections to be used for the overlap removal and𝐸miss
T reconstruction are summarized417
in Table 5.3. We use the RNN-based tau identification, for which we already have a full set of CP418
recommendations for Run-2 dataset. For the “signal” taus in SR/CR/VR,TightRNN identification is used419
on top of the baseline tau (theLooseRNNidentification with loose electron veto). The kinematics of taus420
are also tighten from 20GeVto 200GeV. We do not have eVeto SF forTightRNN identification, but421
agreedwithTauCPgrouptousetheeVetoSFfor MediumRNNworkingpoint. InourSR,electron-faking-tau422
contribution is very small, so no impact on the final result is expected.423
The selection criteria are all implemented in theTauSelectionToolas part of theTauAnalysisTools.424
Documentation can be found in the README-TauSelectionTool.rst.425

6 Signal Region Optimization473
In this section, the optimization of the signal region (SR) is discussed. Two SRs are optimized: one for the474
resonance signal, and the other for the non-resonance signal. The optimal event selection is determined by475
scanning the thresholds for particular kinematic variables, considering the correlation between them, to476
maximize the significance𝑍 [40]:477
𝑍 =
√︄
2(𝑛 ln ( 𝑛(𝑏 + 𝜎2)
𝑏2 + 𝑛𝜎2 ) − 𝑏2
𝜎2 ln 𝑏2 + 𝑛𝜎2
𝑏(𝑏 + 𝜎2) ) (6.1)
where 𝑠 and 𝑏 represent the mean expected signal and background yields, respectively,𝑛 = 𝑠 + 𝑏 and 𝜎 is478
the assumed systematic uncertainty on the background expectation. We assumed𝜎 = 30% in this study.479
Before the scan, some obvious common selections are applied to save the time:480
• 𝐸miss
T > 200 GeV to ensure the plateau of𝐸miss
T trigger efficiency,481
• Number of hadronic𝜏 lepton = 1 (RNNTight),482
• Number of𝑏-jets 𝑁𝑏 = 1 (85% working point,𝑝T > 50GeV),483
• Number of “signal” electrons/muons𝑁ℓ = 0, and484
• min Δ𝜙(𝐸miss

1. The background yields𝑏 > 2;499
2. The yield of a specific SM background process (e.g.,𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈) is set to 0 if it is a negative value.500
The first constraint is set to ensure that we won’t have the zero-background estimation, even though this501
would sacrifice the sensitivity. Also, it ensures that we have a proper minor background estimation for502
the events with the negative weights under tight selection. As a result, the upper bound on the MC stat503
uncertainty is set. Specifically, it’s suppressed to 31% for resonance SR and 40% for non-resonance-SR.504
The numbers in the following sections are updated with the latest signal samples using the corrected505
weights described in Appendix A. Only BSM samples with statistical uncertainties are considered here.506
The interference with SM is expected to be less than 5% of the BSM yields as studied in Appendix I.507

7 Background Estimation Strategy546
The main source of the SM background in this analysis is(𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈)+jets, and top-quark events (𝑡¯𝑡 and547
𝑊𝑡) are subdominant. For these two components, free normalization factors are introduced, as described in548
Section 10. Dedicated CRs are defined to determine the normalization factors and are involved in the fit.549
The data/MC comparison in CRs is discussed in Section 8.550
Events with fake𝜏 lepton (mainly(𝑍 → 𝜈𝜈)+jets for large𝐸miss
T ) are minor source of the SM background551
for this analysis. They are estimated by the simulation directly. Modeling of𝑗 → 𝜏 fake rate in the552
simulation is validated in(𝑍 → ℓℓ)+fake-𝜏 VR in Appendix D. Multi-jet events with both fake𝜏 and553
fake 𝐸miss
T are found to be negligible after the QCD cleaning cuts in Appendix C. The other background554
(diboson, including real𝜏, for which the modeling is relatively reliable) is also estimated by the simulation,555
with conservative uncertainties on the cross sections.556
Concept of the overall background estimation strategy is shown in Figure 7.1.557
WVR1tau
T opVR1tau*
Nb = 0
Nb = 1
(Nτ, Nlep) = (0, 1) (1, 1) (1, 0)
SRT opCR*
WCR
VR0tau
Nb = 2
* For TopCR and TopVR1tau, looser cuts for tau pT, mEt and mT are applied for statistics
Figure 7.1: Definitions of SR, CR and VR of this analysis.
7.1 WCR558
WCR is defined by replacing the𝜏 in the SR to the electron or muon, to take(𝑊 → ℓ𝜈)+jets in the same559
topology( ℓ = 𝑒, 𝜇). Togainthestatistics,andtosuppressthetop-quarkcontributions, 𝑏-jetvetoisrequired560

instead of𝑁𝑏 = 1in the SR. Single lepton triggers are used instead of the𝐸miss
T trigger in the SRs, to avoid561
the bias coming from the muon not included in the𝐸miss

To define the WCRs,𝑚ℓ

other selection cuts are completely the same as the SR, as summarized in Table 7.1.566
For the Non-Resonance WCR, the purity of𝑊+jets is about 90%. It is about 70% for the Resonance WCR,567
and about 20% contribution from diboson events is expected.568

coupling parameter choice is(𝑀LQ, 𝛽23

the CR. Even at the high𝛽23

7.3 Validation Regions588
Estimatednormalizationfactorsfor 𝑊+jetsandtop-quarkeventsarecheckedintheVRs. Wediscussedwith589
LPX conveners about the blinding strategy at the tau+X meeting on 16 May. If the signal contamination590
is less than 10% level, assuming a signal of just about any size that has not been rejected in previous𝜏𝜏591
analysis, basically it is ok to see the data (although we need to check carefully e.g. the contamination of592
𝑊 ′ → 𝜏𝜈 searches).593
The signal contamination is checked by signal samples generated with both leptonic decay and hadronic594
decay 𝜏. For the coupling parameters, we choose(𝑀LQ, 𝛽23
𝐿 ) = (1.5TeV, 0.2) for the Resonance VRs and595
(𝑀LQ, 𝛽23
𝐿 ) = (2.5TeV, 1) for the Non-Resonance VRs, at𝑔𝑈 = 1.5. These parameters are not excluded596
by 𝜏𝜏 search.597

this VR0tau is found to be almost the same as SR.601

8 Data/MC Comparisons618
The ratio of observed data to MC simulation is checked in CRs and VRs for several kinematic variables.619
Only statistical uncertainties are considered. We observed a small discrepancy (<∼ 1.5𝜎) for the data/MC620
ratio between the electron and muon channels in the WCR, but concluded that it is coming from the621
statistical fluctuation. Detailed studies are found in Appendix F. In the final fit, we will combine𝑒 and 𝜇622
channels to increase the statistics, and use the common normalization factors for them.623
8.1 WCR624
WCRs are defined in Section 7.1. The𝑏-tagging requirement is flipped to the𝑏-veto. The lepton and625
leading jet are used instead of𝜏 and 𝑏-jet in the SR.626
8.1.1 Resonance WCR627
The results of data/MC comparison in Resonance WCR are shown in Figure 8.1 and Figure 8.2 in the628
electron and muon channels. The number of observed data is 243 events for the electron channel and 219629
events for the muon channel. The ratios of the observed data events to the MC expectation are1.25 ± 0.09630
in the electron channel and1.08 ± 0.08in the muon channel. No distinct slope is found in each distribution.631
The difference between e- and mu-channels is1.4𝜎.632
8.1.2 Non-Resonance WCR633
The results of data/MC comparison in non-resonance WCR are shown in Figure 8.3 and Figure 8.4 in the634
electron and muon channels. The number of observed data is 474 events for the electron channel and635
398 events for the muon channel. The ratios of observed number of data events to those of simulation636
are 1.00 ± 0.09and 0.80 ± 0.08in the electron and muon channels. The discrepancy between e- and mu-637
channel is1.7𝜎. No distinct slope is found in each distribution.638

9 Systematic Uncertainties685
9.1 Experimental sources686
List of experimental sources of systematic uncertainty considered in this analysis is shown in next pages.687
Symmetrization of (up-down)/2 or (abs(up)+abs(min))/2 is performed for most systematics due to the688
limited statistics of the background in SR, except for JER, mET, and ttbar NNLO uncertainties.689
9.1.1 Luminosity and pileup reweighting690
The uncertainty in the combined 2015–2018 integrated luminosity is 0.83% [41], obtained using the691
LUCID-2 detector [42] for the primary luminosity measurements, complemented by measurements using692
the inner detector and calorimeters.693
The uncertainty associated to the pileup reweighting is followed by prescription in Ref. [43]. The scale694
factor is applied to the data sample, to adjust the𝜇 value for better data/MC agreement on the number of695
vertices distribution after the reweighting. The nominal factor is 1/1.03, with the variation from 1/1.07 to696
1/0.99.697
9.1.2 Tau energy scale and efficiency calibration uncertainties698
The latest 2025 prerecommendation is used with AnalysisBase,25.2.51.699
Tau energy scale (TES)The in-situ measurements using𝑍 → 𝜏𝜏 events (INSITUEXP, FIT) covering700
from low-𝑝T region were performed. Uncertainties at high-𝑝T (DETECTOR, PHYSICSLIST, etc.)701
correspond to old uncertainties derived from test beam and MC comparison, which should still702
be used too. The TES and related uncertainties are provided through the TauSmearingTool with703
“2025-prerec” and “mc20” configurations. The correction is less than 1% with absolute uncertainty704
of less than 1.5%.705
Tau efficiency SFsUncertaintiesonthescalefactorsfortaureconstruction, RNN-basedidentificationand706
eVeto are considered. TauEfficiencyCorrectionTool is configured with “2025-prerec” and “mc20”707
options. Tau reconstruction efficiency uncertainty is TAUS_TRUEHADTAU_EFF_RECO_TOTAL.708
Tau ID efficiencies were estimated for 1-prong and 3-prong taus separately. Uncorrelated NPs are709
assigned for different𝑝T bins. Central value of the SFs is around 1 in all𝑝T bins, with 5–8%710
(1-prong) and 6–11% (3-prong) uncertainties for the Tight ID working point.711
60

ATLAS DRAFT
9.1.3 Jet and𝑬miss
T uncertainties712
The R22 “Phase-1” pre-recommendation is used for JES/JER, pileup subtraction and𝐸miss
T uncertainties.713
CategoryReduction scheme (about 30+5 NPs) is used for JES uncertainties, while FullJER (13+2 NPs)714
scheme for JER.715
MC-based steps of the calibration were derived using R22 MC20 FullSimulation samples. Extra716
uncertainties are added to take into account differences between R21 and R22 (updated topocluster noise717
thresholds and tracking/vertexing changes):718
•JESUnc_VertexingAlg_PreRec719
•JESUnc_Noise_PreRec720
For in-situ calibration, R21 studies are used with extra uncertainties to correct for non-closure when the721
R21 in-situ calibrations are applied to R22 samples:722
• EtaIntercalibration_NonClosure_PreRec723
• InSitu_NonClosure_PreRec724
•JERUnc_Noise_PreRec725
Only for AF3 (signal) samples: extra uncertainty coming from jet response and resolution non-closure726
between FullSim and AF3 is applied.727
• RelativeNonClosure_AF3728
•JERUnc_AF3vsFullSim_AF3_PreRec729
We do NOT use the MC-to-MC calibration option for JES flavor response uncertainty. We do NOT follow730
the JES precision reduced flavor uncertainties prescription, so the Flavor_Response and Composition731
uncertainties are calculated with the default conservative option.732
The JER uncertainties smear both (pseudo-)data and MC events [44]. Shifting a given NP by+1𝜎 will733
correspond with smearing either the (pseudo-)data or MC, while shifting the same NP by−1𝜎 will734
correspond with smearing the MC or (pseudo-)data. If the uncertainty for a given component crosses zero,735
then this will change whether (pseudo)-data or MC is smeared, thus properly retaining the anti-correlated736
behavior implied by the uncertainty, rather than enforcing a correlated behavior. Some more de-737
tails inhttps://twiki.cern.ch/twiki/bin/view/AtlasProtected/JetUncertaintiesRel22#738
Different_smearing_options.739

tion:https://ftag.docs.cern.ch/recommendations/. The Medium eigenvector reduction scheme742

9.2 Theoretical uncertainties754
Since we will just use the single-bin SRs, theoretical uncertainties for the main background components,755
𝑊+jets and top quark events, are estimated only on the “transfer factor”𝑇 𝐹from CR to SRs/VRs. Note756
that we only consider the uncertainty of𝑊+jets (Top) when propagating it from WCR (TopCR) to SR/VR757
due to the high purity of the CRs. Specifically, the contamination from Top backgrounds is 1.6% and 0.4%758
in Resonance and Non-resonance WCRs, respectively. And the contamination from𝑊+jets backgrounds is759
4.8% and 3.4% in Resonance and Non-resonance TopCRs, respectively. Even considering a conservative760
50%uncertainty,thecontributionsfrom 𝑊+jetsandTopbackgroundsinTopCRsandWCRsaremarginal.761
A simplified explanation of the concept here762

9.2.1 𝑾 → 𝝉𝝂+jets781
Following the prescription in PMG twiki page. Sherpa 2.2.11𝑊+jets sample has the internal weights to782
vary the following parameters.783
QCD scale variations7-point QCD scale variations stored as internal weights in the baseline samples,784
from which the envelope around the nominal can be worked out. The scale uncertainty is then given785
bythemaximumshiftoftheenvelopewithrespecttothenominalwiththeversionof ME+PS,which786
is the coherent variation of the matrix element and parton shower renormalization and factorization787
QCDscales. Thisisfoundtohavemoreshifttothenominalthananotherscalevariationof MEonly.788
PDF The Hessian NNPDF3.0 NNLO uncertainty scheme is to be adopted, which can be accessed via the789
LHAPDF tool as it has built-in methods to work out the combined PDF uncertainty. The central790
PDF values for the NNPDF3.0nnlo (PDF261000), MMHT2014nnlo68cl (PDF25300) and CT14nnlo791
(PDF13000) PDF sets are included as variations as well as two NNPDF3.0nnlo𝛼𝑆 variations792
(PDF269000 and PDF270000).793
Electroweak Virtual CorrectionsApproximate NLO EW corrections are included as on-the-fly weights794
usingtheelectroweakvirtualapproximation. TheelectroweakandQCDcomponentscanbecombined795
using an additive, multiplicative, or exponentiated prescription.796
The 10–30% difference (or 30–40% for some generators) of the ATLAS data from SM expectations are797
reported for𝑊/𝑍 plus 𝑏-jets cross sections [1, 45–47]. Since we use the WCRs with𝑏-veto and extrapolate798
the normalization to SRs using the heavy flavor fraction in the MC sample, an additional conservative 20%799
uncertainty is assigned for the resonance and non-resonance regions with𝑏-jets, respectively. A detailed800
study is addressed in the Appendix J. The sensitivity to the signal search is decreased by 2.3% with this801
uncertainty. The discrepancy of the observed data from the SM expectation, discussed in Section 10 and802
Appendix G is relaxed from 2.8 to 2.3𝜎 with this additional uncertainty and other systematics included.803

9.2.3 𝒁 → 𝝂𝝂+fake tau822
The (𝑍 → 𝜈𝜈) plus jet-faking tau is a minor source of the SM background in the SRs. As discussed in823
Appendix D, to take account for the observed mismodeling of fake-tau rate in MC, we assign conservative824
100% uncertainty on its normalization.825

conservative normalization uncertainty of 30%.828

do not have𝛼𝑆 variations for this PDF set. As a result, 10%˜20% uncertainty based on different parameter832
choices is assigned to the signals.833

9.2.6 Summary of theoretical uncertainties considered834
The estimated uncertainties for background samples are summarized in Table 9.1 and Table 9.2.835
Some uncertainties estimated by the alternative MC samples are affected by the statistical fluctuation in the836
tail region. For these uncertainties, smoothing of distributions is applied from the bulk region, to estimate837
the tail distribution reasonably.838
First, the alternative sample is normalized to the nominal sample in the dedicated CR. Then, the𝑚𝑏𝜏839
(resonance) or𝐸miss
T (non-resonance) 𝑁 − 1 distribution is compared between them. The "353QH twice"840
algorithm [48] implemented in ROOT is used to smooth the ratio. Finally, the smoothed ratio is applied to841
the nominal distribution, and the yields in SR/VR at the higher𝑚𝑏𝜏 or 𝐸miss
T are obtained for the given842
systematicuncertainty,whichcorrespondsto (1 ± Δ𝑇 𝐹/𝑇 𝐹). Thisprocedureisperformedforthefollowing843
systematic sources and regions.844
• Resonance regions:845
–Top PS in SR.846
–Top ME in TopVR.847
–Top FSR (down) in SR (relaxed, not smoothed).848
–Top Hdamp in SR (relaxed, not smoothed)849
–𝑡¯𝑡 Recoil in SR (relaxed, not smoothed).850
–Wt interference in TopVR.851
• Non-resonance regions:852
–W+jets QCD Scale (relaxed, not smoothed)853
–Top PS in SR (relaxed, not smoothed).854
–Top ME in SR (relaxed).855
–Top Hdamp in SR (relaxed).856
–𝑡¯𝑡 Recoil in SR (relaxed).857
–Wt interference in SR (relaxed, not smoothed).858
For some uncertainties (marked as “relaxed”), MC statistical uncertainty is too large even with the859
smoothing. Forthem,someselectioncutsarerelaxedtoincreasethestatistics. Weremovedtherequirement860
of the number of jets and require𝑁𝑏 ≥ 1instead of𝑁𝑏 = 1. If the evaluated uncertainty with the looser861
selection cuts is reasonable, smoothing is not applied (marked as “relaxed, not smoothed”). Otherwise,862
both smoothing and relax SR are used (“relaxed”).863
Figures 9.1 and 9.2 show the𝑚𝑏𝜏 and 𝐸miss
T distributions in resonance and non-resonance regions,864
respectively, before and after the smoothing applied.865
The validity of the procedures above are checked in Appendix H.866

10 Statistical Analysis867
10.1 TRexFitter Statistical Framework868
The TRexFitter framework is used to perform the statistical analysis.869
For thebackground-only fit, a maximum-likelihood fit is performed on the 4 CRs to determine the870
normalization factors of𝑊+jets and top-quark events (𝑡¯𝑡 plus single top), each of which has a single bin.871
The post-fit normalization factors and nuisance parameters are applied to SRs and VRs to compare the data872
with the SM expectations.873
For theS+B fit, a maximum-likelihood fit is performed on the 4 CR + 2 SR event categories to determine874
the signal and background normalization factors simultaneously. The signal acceptance in each category is875
predicted by the simulation.876
The likelihood functionL (𝜇, ®𝜆, ®𝜃) depends on:877
• 𝜇: the signal strength,878
• ®𝜆 = {𝜆Res
𝑊 , 𝜆Non-Res
𝑊 , 𝜆Res
𝑡 , 𝜆Non-Res
𝑡 }: the background normalization factors for𝑊+jets and top-quark879
samples, separated for Resonance and Non-Resonance regions, and880
• ®𝜃: a set of nuisance parameters to take account for systematic uncertainties in the fit.881
The correlation between BSM and Interference terms of the signal sample is considered by normalizing the882
Interference sample by the factor√𝜇. The number of signal and background yields in each SR and CR is883
described by:884
𝑁𝑒𝑥 𝑝 = 𝜇𝑁 𝑠𝑖𝑔 (𝜃) + √𝜇𝑁 𝑖𝑛𝑡 (𝜃) + 𝜆𝑊 𝑁 𝑀𝐶
𝑊 (𝜃) + 𝜆𝑡 𝑁 𝑀𝐶
𝑡 (𝜃) + 𝑁 𝑀𝐶
𝑜𝑡 ℎ𝑒𝑟 𝑠(𝜃), (10.1)
where the appropriate normalization factor (𝜆Res or 𝜆Non-Res) is applied depending on the category. The885
likelihood is defined as:886
L =
Ö
𝑆𝑅,𝐶 𝑅
Pois(𝑁𝑜𝑏𝑠 |𝑁𝑒𝑥 𝑝) ×
Ö
Gaus(𝜃|0, 1). (10.2)
A very limited effect on the exclusion limit is expected according to the study in Appendix I. For the887
background-only fit, the signal strength𝜇 is fixed to 0.888
Both 𝜇 and ®𝜆 are treated as free parameters in the fit. The®𝜃 allows variations of the expectations for signal889
and background according to the systematic uncertainties, subject to Gaussian, log-normal or Poisson890
constraints in the likelihood fit. Their fitted values represent the deviations from the nominal expectations891
thatgloballyprovidethebestfittothedata. Statisticaluncertaintiesineachbinduetothelimitedsizeofthe892
simulated background samples are taken into account by dedicated parameters using the Beeston-Barlow893
“lite” (one parameter per bin) technique [49].894
73

ATLAS DRAFT
The test statistic is evaluated with the RooFit package [50]:895
𝑞 𝜇 = −2 ln L (𝜇, ®𝜆 𝜇, ®𝜃 𝜇)
L ( ˆ𝜇, ˆ®𝜆, ˆ®𝜃)
, (10.3)
where ˆ𝜇, ˆ®𝜆 and ˆ®𝜃 are the best-fit𝜇, ®𝜆 and ®𝜃 to maximize the likelihood, and®𝜆 𝜇 and ®𝜃 𝜇 are the best-fit®𝜆 and896
®𝜃 with the fixed signal strength𝜇.897
The uncertainty onˆ𝜇, Δ ˆ𝜇, is evaluated by scanning over𝜇 to find the range corresponding to𝑞 𝜇 < 1. An898
estimation of the contribution from systematic uncertainties is found by subtracting in quadrature fromΔ ˆ𝜇899
the statistical uncertainty, which is determined by redoing the fit to data after fixing all NPs to their best-fit900
values. The impact of each nuisance parameter is evaluated by repeating the fit with the parameter fixed to901
its post-fit value±1𝜎, while allowing all other parameters to float. The resulting shift inˆ𝜇 with respect to902
the nominal fit is taken as the impact of that nuisance parameter.903
Totestthenullhypothesis,theteststatistics 𝑞0 at 𝜇 = 0isparticularlyimportant. Ifnosignalsexceedingthe904
SM background are observed, the upper limit on the signal strength𝜇 is derived using the CLs method [51]905
at each parameter point of the LQ model.906
Both shape and normalization systematics pruning can be performed during the fit. The "shape" pruning907
firstlynormalizesthealternativedistributiontothesameyieldasthenominaldistribution. Ifthealternative908
distribution differs from the nominal distribution by less than the threshold in each bin, the "shape"909
component will be pruned. Since we are using 1-bin estimation for each region, all NPs are shape-dropped.910
The normalization systematic pruning requires that the non-prunable systematics be above the threshold.911
Currently, we set the threshold to 1% to simplify the fit model.912
10.2 Background-only fit results913
Thebackground-onlyfittotheCRswithallsystematicuncertaintiescurrentlyavailablehasbeenperformed.914
The fit result of the normalization factors shown in Figure 10.1 is then extrapolated to the SRs and VRs.915
NoticethatthenormalizationfactorsforTopbackgroundsareingoodagreement,whereasthoseforW+jets916
are incompatible. This is considered due to the slope observed in the jet𝑝T distribution in Section 8.1.917
The summary of observed data in each VR compared with the pre-fit and the post-fit expectation are shown918
in Figure 10.2 and Table 10.1. Except for the non-resonance VR0tau, all VRs show good agreement within919
1𝜎. A detailed investigation for this specific region is performed in Appendix G. The difference of data920
from the expectation divided by total statistical and systematic uncertainties is2.3𝜎, and it seems to be a921
statistical fluctuation.922
Additional study to check the0𝑏 → 1𝑏 extrapolation with the mass or𝑚T reverted 1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 VRs is also923
performed in Appendix M. As a result, we observed good agreement between data and MC prediction in924
this region, which indicates that the extrapolation is reasonable.925

After the LPX subgroup approval, we are allowed to see the SR data. The unblinded results are shown958
in this chapter. The signals used for the results are already updated with the final version of the signal959
samples, which use the default weight instead of additional internal weights.960
11.1 B-only fit961

Figure 11.1. The results are very similar to the background-only fit results in the CR shown in Figure 10.1,963
indicating that the background estimation worked as expected. As summarized in the table 10.1, the964

SRs, respectively. We observed 3 and 2 events in the resonance and the non-resonance SRs, which agree966
very well with the SM expectations. The pull plots are shown in Figure 11.2. No obvious constraint967
observed. The correlation matrix is also shown in Figure 11.3. The summary plot with the data points in968
the SRs is shown in Figure 11.4. Unblinded SR distribution is shown in Figure 11.5969

11.4 Toy limits999
The toy limits are also performed due to the low statistics in the SRs. The number of toys is 10k, and the1000
scan steps are 15 for each parameter point of the signals. The scan range of𝜇 is optimized, where the1001
minimum 𝜇 is0, andthemaximum 𝜇 isdecidedbasedonthe +2𝜎 expectationoftheasymptoticlimit. The1002
resultscomparedwithasymptoticlimitsareshowninFigures11.14and11.15. Theasymptoticexpectation1003
agrees well with the toy limits.1004

12 Post ATLAS circulation updates1005
During the ATLAS circulation, a large contamination was pointed out in the current WVRs from the large1006
𝛽23
𝐿 signals. It was overlooked since we were looking at the low𝛽23
𝐿 scenarios when we checked the signal1007
contamination in the VRs, due to the delay of the signal production. There are no significant excess is1008
observed in the WVRs. At the PAM, we agreed with the PC and conveners to add the WVRs in the fit as1009
0b SRs. The additional studies regarding that are described in this section. Hereafter, WVR is denoted as1010
SR0b.1011
12.1 Signal diagram for the𝝉𝝂 final states1012
The study with truth information is performed to check what kind of signals are contaminated in SR0b.1013
ThePartonTruthLabelIDof truth jet is used for identifying the truth quark flavour of the leading jet, and1014
the Figure 12.1 shows the composition in SR0b-Res and SR0b-NonRes at𝑀LQ = 1.5TeV, 𝑔𝑈 = 1.5and1015
𝛽23
𝐿 = 0.6. In SR0b-Res, 30% of the events are coming from the process containing a𝑠 quark, and 60%1016
from a𝑐 quark. In SR0b-NonRes, 33% of the events are coming from non-resonance𝜏𝜈 with ISR𝑠-jet,1017
and 11% of the events are coming from the ISR𝑐-jet. Others are coming from𝑠𝑐 → 𝜏𝜈 + ISR gluon jet.1018

12.2 Interference study and signal model in 0b SR1024
InSR0b,theinterferenceeffectwiththeSM 𝜏𝜈 processesislargerthanSRswitha 𝑏-jet,becauseintheSM1025
the production of a𝜏𝜈 system with an associated𝑏 quark is CKM-suppressed making the SM amplitude1026
that interferes with new physics contribution highly reduced at the high-mass region. Since there are1027
non-negligible impact from the interference to SR0bs, the fractions of the interference contribution to the1028
pureBSMyieldsarecomputedateachpointofthecouplingparameterscanandsummarizedinFigure12.6.1029
Its impact is up to 20% at𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV and 30~40% at higher LQ masses. It is confirmed that the1030
interference effect does not cause the negative signal yields at the range of the coupling constants sensitive1031
to this analysis. The interference effect is taken into account in the fit, by normalizing the interference term1032
by √𝜇 as discussed in Section. 10.1.1033
12.3 Results with updated 0b + 1b SRs1034
Since we do not change the selection cuts to define the SR0b, and the systematics uncertainties are already1035
estimated for them, we simply include the SR0b to the likelihood of the fit. The B-only fit results, using1036
CRs only in the likelihood, are the same as before.1037

12.4 tautau signal contamination1053
We checked how many𝜏𝜏 signals (DSID: 605435~605602) in the same𝑈1 model are contaminated in our1054
signal regions. These samples are generated with𝛽33
𝑅 = 0, 𝛽33
𝐿 = 1and 𝛽23
𝐿 = 0. To compare with these𝜏𝜏1055
signals, we picked up available𝜏𝜈 samples with parameter (𝑀LQ, 𝑔𝑈, 𝛽23
𝐿 ) near the exclusion limit. One1056
is a low𝛽23
𝐿 signal at low𝑀LQ, whose (𝑀LQ, 𝑔𝑈, 𝛽23
𝐿 )=(1.8 TeV, 3, 0.6) and another is a high𝛽23
𝐿 signal1057
at high𝑀LQ, at(𝑀LQ, 𝑔𝑈, 𝛽23
𝐿 ) = (3TeV, 2, 1.4). For𝜏𝜏 signals, we choose the samples generated with1058
(𝑀LQ, 𝑔𝑈, 𝛽23
𝐿 ) = (1.8TeV, 2.8, 0) and (3TeV, 2.1, 0). To make a fair comparison with the same𝛽23
𝐿 , we1059
calculated the𝜏𝜏 signal cross-section as a function of𝛽23
𝐿 using MadGraph, and then scaled the yields1060
from the actual𝜏𝜏 signals with the factor of𝜎𝛽23
𝐿 =0.6/𝜎𝛽23
𝐿 =0 = 2.7 and 𝜎𝛽23
𝐿 =1.0/𝜎𝛽23
𝐿 =0 = 30.1061
Table 12.1 summarises the possible𝜏𝜏 signal contamination in our SRs at𝛽23
𝐿 = 0 and yields scaled to the1062
corresponding 𝛽23
𝐿 . Weobservedthattheoverall 𝜏𝜏 contaminationcanbearound35%inSR1b,and~5%in1063
SR0b. A possible 35% under-estimation of the signal yields is a bit larger than the theoretical uncertainties1064
we currently consider (about 20%). We further estimated the possible impact on the sensitivity from1065
the 𝜏𝜏 contamination. We scaled the nominal signal yields in SRs by additional constant normalisation1066
factors, and reperformed the exclusion fit. The normalisation factors refer to the ratio of𝜏𝜏 and 𝜏𝜈 yields1067
in Table 12.1. Specifically, we applied factors of 1.37, 1.33, 1.05 and 1.07 to the signal yields in SR1b-Res,1068
SR1b-NonRes, SR0b-Res and SR0b-NonRes, respectively.1069
Asaresults,thelimitonthesignalstrength 𝜇isimprovedby8%~14%dependingonthe 𝑀LQ,butthesignal1070
strength is scaled to𝑔4
𝑈 and 𝛽232
𝐿 , so the improvement on the coupling constants is not so significant. We1071
recalculatedthe 𝑔𝑈 limitandtheresultsaresummarisedintheTables12.2and12.3. Possibleimprovements1072
are about 5% at 1.8TeVand about 2% at 3TeV. Therefore, we would like to conclude that the missing𝜏𝜏1073
contribution gives the under-estimate of our sensitivity, but mostly covered by the theoretical uncertainty1074
we are currently considering, and the possible improvement on the coupling constant is 2–5% depending1075
on the mass.1076

12.5 Interpretation with right-handed LQ signals1077
Currently, we only have interference samples for 1.5TeVright-handed signals. It was fine at the previous1078
round of the analysis using SR1b only, since the interference effect is marginal, but as discussed above, it is1079
not negligible in SR0b.1080
We have several options for this part, to be decided.1081
1. keep the visible cross-section limit discussion in the paper without considering interference.1082
2. study the interference effect at 1.5 TeV and apply it to the other masses1083
3. or, just drop the discussion of right-handed interpretation in the paper, as well as Figure 6 and Table1084
8 in the auxiliary material.1085
For the 2nd option, at 1.5TeV, the interference effects are compared between pure left-handed and1086
right-handed scenarios. The ratio of interference is summarized in the Table 12.4. We found that the1087
fraction is very close for|𝛽33
𝑅 | = 0 and 1 in resonance SR and in non-resonance SR with the coefficient1088
2.65. Further study needs interference samples with other coupling parameters.1089

13 Conclusion1093
A first search for high-mass leptoquark signals with ATLAS is presented, targeting final states with a1094
hadronically-decaying 𝜏 lepton, a𝑏-jet and missing transverse momentum. Two signal regions are defined:1095
one for the “resonance” signals, corresponding to a single production of the leptoquark decaying to𝑏𝜏 in1096
associationwithaneutrino,andtheotherforthe“non-resonance”ones,consideringthe 𝑡-channelexchange1097
of the leptoquark, which manifests as an excess in the high-𝑚T tail of𝜏 and 𝐸miss
T . After the high-𝑝T 𝜏1098
and 𝐸miss
T requirements, the fake tau contribution is strongly suppressed, and the main source of the SM1099
background is(𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈)+jets and top-quark events containing real𝜏 leptons. Dedicated control regions1100
are defined for these backgrounds, and included in a profile-likelihood fit to give a constraint on their1101
normalizations.1102
In resonance and non-resonance SRs, 3 and 2 events are observed with the background expectations1103
2.69 ± 0.71 and 1.88 ± 0.65. Given that no significant excess is observed in both SRs, the upper limits1104
on the𝑈1 vector leptoquark couplings are set at 95% CL. For the pure left-handed scenario with𝛽33
𝑅 = 0,1105
the observed (expected) upper limits on𝑔𝑈 are 0.71(0.68) at 𝛽23
𝐿 = 2.0 and 2.84(2.81) at 𝛽23
𝐿 = 0.2 for1106
leptoquark mass of 1.5TeV, and1.42(1.40) at 𝛽23
𝐿 = 2.0 and 4.83(4.78) at 𝛽23
𝐿 = 0.2 for leptoquark mass1107
of 2.5 TeV. The results tested part of the region compatible with the𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) anomaly at𝛽23
𝐿 > 1.8.1108

A Signal Validations1262
A.1 Test samples for the initial study1263
Two mass points of 1.5TeVand 2.5TeVwere generated for the initial studies, using configurations1264
e8531 (rel 23.6.10), and DAODs are listed in Table A.2. The 1.5TeVsamples were processed with both1265
Geant4 (s3767) and AF3 (a907) but the 2.5TeVwas only with AF3. In the event generation, parameters of1266
the matrices in Eq. 4.1 were set to𝑔𝑈 = 1 and 𝛽23
𝐿 = 0.2. The 𝛽33
𝑅 was set to 0. The setting parameters are1267
summarized in Table A.1. The tau is specified to be only hadronically decayed to enhance the signal events.1268
The BSM events were further separated by BFilter/BVeto filters which checked the𝑏-quark existence after1269
Pythia: when 𝑏-quarks with|𝜂| <5 (any𝑝T) are found, events are filtered/rejected, respectively. For the1270
interference events, the sum of neutrino’s momenta in the ME (MadGraph), which is callednet, was used1271
since we found a destructive effect of the interference term was mitigated at high-𝐸miss
T region. We used1272
net> 150GeV/< 150GeV, which corresponded tomissetand missetmaxin the MadGraph. Note that1273
thesevariablesdon’tincludethemomentaofneutrinosfromtau-decay,whichisperformedinthePythiastep.1274
Only at𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV, the additional “inclusive” samples not separated into BSM and Interference were1275
generated for the validation. 10M events were generated for each sample. There are internal event weights1276
to vary𝑔𝑈, 𝛽23
𝐿 and 𝑀LQ parameters, so that we can scan them in the interpretation. The ranges of𝑔𝑈, 𝛽23
𝐿 ,1277
and 𝑀LQ are {0.5, 1, 1.5, 2.5, 3.5}, {0, 0.2, 0.6, 1, 1.4}, and{1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4, 5} (TeV), respectively. The1278
other couplings were set to 0 since they are irrelevant to our signal (the𝜅 parameter is also set to 0, so for1279
now we can test𝑈Yang-Mills
1 model only).1280
Figure A.1 shows some validation plots for the BSM part of the signals. The “Baseline” cuts for SRs1281
in Table 6.4 are applied, using the truth tau, truth lepton, truth jets and truth𝐸miss
T . The following set1282
of parameters is used:𝑔𝑈 = 1.5/2.5, 𝛽23
𝐿 = 1, 𝛽33
𝑅 = 0 and 𝜅 = 0. Here, samples are separated into1283
“resonance” and “non-resonance” components by checking the parent particle’s PDG ID of𝜏 in the final1284
state. If it is the LQ, the event is categorized into the resonance part, otherwise non-resonance. For the1285
resonance signals, the edge of the peak is found at𝑚𝑏𝜏 = 1.5 TeVand 2.5TeV, which corresponds to1286
the LQ mass, due to the neutrinos in the tau lepton decay. On the other hand, the𝑏𝜏 mass is decreased1287
exponentially for the non-resonance part, not depending on the LQ mass.1288
Figure A.2 shows some validation plots for the Interference part of the signals at𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV. Here,1289
the signal events are separated into the𝑏-veto and𝑏-tagged regions. The interference effect is small for the1290
resonance part of the signals (consistent with zero). For the non-resonance signals, if we do not require any1291
𝑏-jets in the events, a strong destructive interference effect is observed at𝑚T < 800GeVregion, due to the1292
large contribution of the SM𝑊 → 𝜏𝜈. After the requirement of𝑏-jets in the event, the destructive effect1293
of the interference is mitigated, thanks to the small CKM matrix elements𝑉𝑏𝑐 and 𝑉𝑏𝑢 and suppressed1294
𝑊+jets events, and it is found only at𝑚T < 400 GeV. In the SRs with high-𝑝T 𝜏 (> 200 GeV), high𝐸miss
T1295
(> 400 GeV) and high𝑚T (> 600 GeV), the interference effect is marginal.1296

About 30% of the signals have a𝑐-jet in the event. For the𝑐-jet production via the LQ model assumed, the1297
initial state must be a𝑏 quark, so its contribution is relatively small. It is not included in the scope of this1298
analysis, and we only focus on the𝜏𝜈𝑏 final state.1299
There were several issues in the test samples:1300
•We have the internal weights to vary the leptoquark mass. It works fine for the non-resonant1301
componentofthesignals, butdoesn’tworkwellfortheresonancepartduetothepoorstatisticsatthe1302
tail (it’s quite difficult to reproduce e.g. 2TeVresonance by reweighting 1.5TeVsample). For the1303
final interpretation, we need to generate samples with each mass point at 1.5TeV, 2TeV, 2.5TeV,1304
3 TeV, 4 TeV and 5 TeV.1305
• Events rejected by the BFilter/BVeto filters after Pythia are necessary to use the internal weights1306
since MadGraph calculates the internal weights using all the produced events. Any events produced1307
by MadGraph should be kept in order to use the internal weights. So, we have to remove the1308
BFilter/BVeto filters.1309
•We would reweight the nominal sample with𝛽33
𝑅 = 0 to |𝛽33
𝑅 | = 11, but it might not work well.1310
Therefore, forthefinalinterpretations, weneedatleasttwosetsofsamplesfor 𝛽33
𝑅 = 0and −1ateachmass1311
point of{1.5, 1.8, 2, 2.5, 3} TeV. On the other hand, if we need to interpret our results with the minimal1312
model, we need samples with𝜅 = 0 having the full scan of𝑔𝑈 and 𝛽23
𝐿 .1313
1 The choice of sign for𝛽33
𝑅 is important for low-energy observables, like𝑅(𝐷 (∗) ) anomalies, but not for the signatures at the
LHC. The reason for this is that the new physics amplitudes of different chiralities do not interfere with each other and the
amplitude proportional to𝛽33
𝐿 𝛽33
𝑅 does not interfere with the SM ones [37]. Therefore we generate the samples with𝛽33
𝑅 = +1,
but it can be used for𝛽33
𝑅 = −1 which is preferred by low-energy observables, and collectively described as|𝛽33
𝑅 | = 1.

A.2 Validation of internal event weights for parameter scan1314
Detailsarediscussedin https://indico.cern.ch/event/1353419/contributions/5700345/attachments/1315
2764781/4815668/MG_VLQ_taunujet_MCrequest.pdf.1316
For the limit setting, we use a single sample generated with𝑔𝑈 = 1 and 𝛽23
𝐿 = 0.2 at each mass point,1317
and vary these parameters using the MadGraph internal event weights. To validate the reliability of the1318
weights, we also prepared a set of independent “reference” samples generated with the varied parameters.1319
Cross sections and selected kinematic distributions are compared between the weighted and the reference1320
samples.1321
TheratioofcrosssectionsfortheweightedsamplestothoseforthereferencesamplesisshowninFigureA.3.1322
The definition of indices is summarized in Table A.3. The “baseline” parameters𝑔𝑈 = 1.0 and 𝛽23
𝐿 = 0.21323
correspond to the index 9.1324
Overall, the weighting method reproduces the reference well. The difference between the weighted and1325
reference samples is typically less than 10%, particularly in the range1.0 < 𝑔 𝑈 < 4.0. At larger couplings1326
(𝑔𝑈 > 2.0), the method does not work for𝛽23
𝐿 = 0.0, and there is a systematic trend of overestimating1327
the cross section at higher𝛽23
𝐿 values, which becomes more pronounced as𝑔𝑈 increases. At the lowest1328
coupling (𝑔𝑈 = 0.5), a different trend is observed: the cross sections obtained from the weighting method1329
are up to a factor of two larger than those from the ref samples at𝛽23
𝐿 < 0.2.1330
At the parameter points where discrepancies in the cross sections are observed, differences are also found1331
in several kinematic distributions. In these cases, the reweighting with the internal event weights alone1332
cannot adequately absorb the differences between the resonance and non-resonance components. This1333
also suggests that a one-dimensional reweighting using only the jet𝑝T or the𝑚𝑏𝜏 is insufficient, since1334
it is difficult to simultaneously correct both components across different mass points. To address this,1335
we propose an additional reweighting at the truth level using the two-dimensional distribution of the1336
leading-jet 𝑝T and the𝑚𝑏𝜏. First, the ratio of cross sections between the weighted and reference samples1337
is applied as a global scale factor to adjust the overall normalization. Then, the ratio of weighted to1338
reference samples is computed bin-by-bin in the two-dimensional histogram, and this is used to correct1339
the shape. With these two variables, the resonance and non-resonance components can be effectively1340
adjusted in a pseudo-independent manner. All the necessary variables are included in DAOD_PHYS.1341
With this additional reweighting, we checked the number of signal events at each step of the cut flow,1342
mimicking the SR selections with truth-level observables. We found that, at every step, the difference1343
between the weighted and reference samples is less than 10% for both resonance and non-resonance SRs,1344
which is smaller than the signal theoretical uncertainties. An example of the additional weights is shown in1345
Figure A.4.1346
The expected limits with and without the additional correction above are compared in Figure A.5. The1347
sensitivity is significantly decreased when𝑔𝑈 becomes large due to the over-estimation of the resonance1348
signals.1349

B Complementarity with𝝉𝝉 Searches1350
This section is not ready for review.1351
The previous ATLAS analyses for the non-resonant LQ was based on the𝜏𝜏 fianl states [28, 29]. Based1352
on the discussion in Section 3, they assumed their signal strength depends only on𝑔𝑈 parameter for the1353
simplicity, with the small𝛽23
𝐿 motivated by UV completion assumptions. To confirm these assumptions,1354
the TRUTH DAODs are produced for𝑝 𝑝 → 𝜏𝜏 events using the same jobOptions for this analysis. The1355
parameters 𝑔𝑈 and 𝛽23
𝐿 are varied and checked the selection efficiencies for𝜏𝜏 analysis [29] at each point,1356
using the truth taus and jets.1357
Results will be added later.1358

C QCD Cleaning Cuts1359
The QCD multijet background is studied in this appendix using the simulation samples. For the signal1360
topologies shown in Figure 2.3, the possible source of the multijet background is the event with a jet faking1361
𝜏 andthemis-reconstructionof 𝐸miss
T . Therequirementofhigh 𝐸miss
T canrejectmostofthemulti-jetevents,1362
but due to the large cross-section, a few can still remain in the SR. The simulated dijet events in the SR are1363
carefully checked, and as shown in the figure C.1, we found that the𝐸miss
T in the remaining events comes1364
from a mis-measurement of the high-energy hadronic jet. These events have a smallΔ𝜙 between 𝐸miss
T and1365
a jet. Meanwhile, the real𝐸miss
T from the LQ signal does not have this kind of tendency.1366
Figure C.1: Diagram of𝐸miss
T from LQ signal (left) and multi-jet events (right).
The QCD cleaning study has been performed with a minimum set of selection cuts defined in Table C.1, to1367
avoid the statistical fluctuation. Themin Δ𝜙 distributions of signal and the dijet simulation events after the1368
event selections are shown in Figure C.2. It shows that the multijet backgrounds are enriched in the small1369
region ofmin Δ𝜙, while the relatively flat distribution is found for signals. A cut onmin Δ𝜙 is effective to1370
reduce the multijet background. In addition, the fraction of the fake𝐸miss

D Validation of𝒁+fake-tau contribution1387
A possible source of fake𝜏 contributions in the SRs, which require high𝐸miss
T , is 𝑍 → 𝜈𝜈+jets. Its1388
contribution in each SR is 10% or less, so it is estimated by the MC simulation directly with conservative1389
uncertainties. Since the modeling of the fake𝜏 rate is not reliable in the simulation, the ZVR is defined1390
to study it. The ZVR requires two “signal” muons/electrons and exactly one hadronic tau. The dilepton1391
mass is required to be consistent with the𝑍-boson mass. To ensure the data statistics, a𝑏-veto and looser1392
selection cuts than the SRs are applied, as summarized in Table D.1. Both electron and muon channels are1393
utilized to further increase the data statistics. The𝑍-boson 𝑝T is, as with the SR, reconstructed by jet and1394
tau 𝑝T, i.e. | ®𝑝𝑙𝑙
T + ®𝑝𝑚𝑖𝑠𝑠
T |, to subtract the muon/electron contribution from the𝐸miss
T . On top of that, a loose1395
cut on𝑝𝑙𝑙
T is applied to suppress𝑡¯𝑡 background and increase the purity of𝑍+jets.1396

contribution to the SRs is minor. Therefore, we adopt the MC-based estimation for𝑍+jets and assign an1404
additional 100% uncertainty to account for the observed mismodeling.1405

E Sensitivity projection to Run 3 and HL-LHC1406
As discussed in Section 3, the signal strength scales as𝑔4
𝑈 𝛽2
23. Therefore, the sensitivity to the couplings is1407
expected to improve only slowly with increasing integrated luminosity.1408
The expected limits on𝛽23
𝐿 and 𝑔𝑈 for different integrated luminosities of 140fb−1 (Run-2 only), 450fb−11409
(Run-2 + Run-3) and 3000fb−1 (HL-LHC) are shown in Figure E.1. The benchmark mass points at1410
𝑀LQ = 2TeVand3 TeVareshown. Thecenter-of-massenergyisfixedat13 TeV. AndAsimovdataisused1411
in all regions. The selection cuts are not reoptimized for the higher luminosity scenario. The improvement1412
on the limits by adding Run-3 data is about1𝜎. Even with 3000fb−1, it is about2𝜎 level. Note that here1413
weinterpolatethe 𝜇 = 1lineinbetweenthegridstodrawthelimitwithROOTfunction,whereasthesignal1414
strength 𝜇 is proportional to the fourth power of the coupling𝑔𝑈. Therefore the actual difference of the1415
limits between Run-2, Run-2+Run-3 and HL-LHC should be a bit smaller than 1 or 2𝜎. Of course we can1416
further optimize the selections, and a small gain is expected by increasing the center-of-mass energy to1417
13.6 or 14TeV, but with this approach, a careful step-by-step verification of the𝐵-anomaly favored region1418
will be necessary over the next 10-20 years.1419

F Control region study for e/𝝁 difference1420
This appendix introduces a detailed study of the control region used to determine the normalization of1421
𝑊+jets (WCRs). As the results shown in the Section 8, we observed the discrepancy between e- and1422
mu-channels in the data/MC ratios. Especially for Non-Resonance WCR, the data/MC ratio is1.00 ± 0.091423
fortheelectronchannel,while 0.80 ± 0.08forthemuonchannel,correspondingtothe 1.7 𝜎 discrepancy.1424
To investigate this, we first carefully checked the all relevant MC corrections, separately applied in1425
the e and mu channels, e.g. trigger efficiency SF, and confirmed they are correctly applied. We then1426
checked the data/MC ratio at each step of the cut flow. The discrepancy appears after applying the cut1427
50 < 𝑝 𝑗𝑒𝑡
T < 250GeVrequirementforNon-ResonanceWCR.Thedata-to-dataandMC-to-MCcomparisons1428
on jet𝑝T distribution between e- and mu-channels are performed, and shown in Figure F.1. The e- and1429
mu-channels are generally consistent in both data and MC. The e-channel has slightly higher yields in the1430
data and slightly less in the MC, but within the statistical uncertainty.1431
Anupwardfluctuationisobservedintheelectronchannelindataaround80–100 GeV. ForMC,wechecked1432
the event weight distribution, including cross-section, efficiency SFs, pileup weight etc., for electron and1433
muon channels, as a function of jet𝑝T. As shown in Figure F.2, some events have abnormally large weight,1434
around 30 for a single event. These large weighted events are mainly distributed in𝑝 𝑗𝑒𝑡
T < 50 GeV, but1435
sometimes, (maybe) due to pileup overlay conditions, randomly found in higher𝑝T region. In our WCR,1436
they are not found in electron channel, but there is one event in muon channel at𝑝T > 50GeV. This makes1437
the MC yields in the muon channel higher than the electron channel.1438
In summary, the observed discrepancy arises from fluctuations in electron data and muon MC, both likely1439
statistical in nature. To improve statistical precision, we will combine the electron and muon channels1440
and use a common normalization factor in the final fit. To ensure the robustness of this approach, we will1441
validate the modeling by carefully checking the post-fit data/MC agreement in the validation regions.1442

G Validation region study1443
This section investigates the post-fit discrepancy between data and MC observed in the Non-Resonant1444
VR0tau, as discussed in Section 10.2. The difference of observed data from the SM expectation is2.84𝜎1445
when including all systematic uncertainties currently available. It is reduced to about2.5𝜎 if we include1446
the additional 20% uncertainty on𝑊+jets heavy flavor modeling uncertainty as discussed in Section 9.1447
Furthermore, thediscrepancy is reducedto about2.3𝜎 if we includethe theory uncertainties that cannotbe1448
canceled between SRs/VRs and CRs as we summarized in Table 9.1 and Table 9.2.1449
The pre-fit data/MC ratio in each step of the cutflow (table 7.1) is shown in Figure G.1. The data/MC1450
ratio remains close to unity after the jet multiplicity requirement. The discrepancy arises after applying1451
𝐸miss
T > 400 GeV. The 𝐸miss
T N-1 distribution in VR0tau is shown in Figure G.2. The data generally agree1452
well with the MC, except for a specific𝐸miss
T range between 425 GeV and 450 GeV.1453
If we loosen the𝐸miss
T cut to 300GeV, the data/MC discrepancy is relaxed to 1.8𝜎 (stat-only). Conversely,1454
if we tighten the𝐸miss
T cut to 500GeV, the discrepancy is also improved to 1.5𝜎 (stat-only). This suggests1455
that the excess is localized within a narrow𝐸miss
T range.1456
FigureG.3showsthelepton 𝑝Tdistributionintherange 425 < 𝐸 miss
T < 450GeV. Thedataexcessisobserved1457
only in 300–400GeVbin, so it is not coming from the threshold effect of the lepton𝑝T cut (> 200 GeV).1458
We examined the 12 data events in this region (425 < 𝐸 miss
T < 450 GeV, 300 < 𝑝 ℓ
T < 400 GeV), which1459
consist of 7 in the electron channel and 5 in the muon channel. Figure G.4 shows the𝜂–𝜙 map of the1460
lepton and𝑏-jet. These events are not clustered in any specific detector region, suggesting no localized1461
detector-related issue.1462
In a nutshell, the excess appears to be a statistical fluctuation. The probability of observing a2.3𝜎1463
discrepancy in at least one out of six independent VRs is approximately 20% (1 − (96.4%)6).1464

H Validation for theory uncertainties estimation.1465
As described in Section 9, we estimated some theoretical systematic uncertainties for the background1466
process with smoothing or loose selection cuts to mitigate the impact of the MC statistical uncertainty.1467
This section discusses the validity of those methods to estimate the uncertainty reasonably.1468
Toestimatesomeuncertainties, werelaxedtherequirementsofjetand 𝑏-jetmultiplicities. Ifthesystematic1469
uncertainty to be estimated is not correlated to the number of jets and𝑏-jets, it is fine to use it to estimate1470
the systematic uncertainty for the actual SRs/VRs. For TopISR uncertainty in the resonance SR, we have1471
enough MC statistics and we do not need to relax any selection cuts. We tried to relax jet multiplicity1472
requirementinthatregion,andre-estimatetheuncertainty. TheresultisshowninFigureH.1. Theresulting1473
uncertainty 1 + Δ𝑇 𝐹/𝑇 𝐹 is changed from 0.96 to 0.92. The difference between the results for SR and1474
relaxed SR is less than 5%, which is acceptable compared with the statistical errors of 0.29.1475
Figure H.1: Comparison of systematics and nomianal N-1 distribution of𝑀𝜏+𝑏 in the Resonance SR (left) and𝑁 𝑗𝑒𝑡 𝑠
relaxed Resoannce SR (right). The uncertainties for the two cases are -0.04 and -0.08, respectively.
The validity of applying smoothing for the N-1 distribution is checked for the Top ME uncertainty in1476
resonance TopVR. Some selection cuts are relaxed to increase the statistics:𝑁𝑏 >= 2instead of𝑁𝑏 = 2;1477
and the jet multiplicity requirement is removed. The N-1 distributions with and without smoothing are1478
shown in Figure H.2. The estimated uncertainties are found to be0.27 ± 0.10and 0.15 ± 0.12before and1479
after the smoothing, respectively. The results are consistent between each other, which indicates that the1480
smoothing is working as expected.1481

I Study for the Signal interference with SM1482
This section studies the interference contribution in the SRs. For now, the interference sample is available1483
only for𝑀LQ = 1.5 TeV. The interference contribution at different(𝛽23
𝐿 , 𝑔𝑈) sets is obtained using the1484
internal weights. The ratio of𝑁𝑖𝑛𝑡 /𝑁𝐵𝑆 𝑀 in a 2-dimensional plane of𝛽23
𝐿 –𝑔𝑈 is shown in figure I.1.1485
The Resonance-only and Non-resonance-only limits are also presented in the plots. In the resonance1486
SR, the interference contribution is typically< 5% at all parameter points. In the non-resonance SR,1487
its contribution is up-to 40% at the lowest𝑔𝑈 region, but the signal yields at that region are quite low1488
and cannot be excluded by the current analysis. At the larger coupling, around the 95% CL limit by the1489
non-resonance SR alone, the interference contribution is typically less than 10% at𝑀LQ = 1.5TeV. At the1490
low-mass region at 1.5TeV, the sensitivity of the non-resonance SR is low compared to the resonance SR.1491
The expected 95% CL limit compared with BSM-only fit is shown in Figure I.2. The interference is small1492
enough to be ignored during the fit. At 3 TeV, where the non-resonance SR is dominant, the interference1493
contribution is found to be less than 5 % around the limit.1494
By fitting with the final signal samples, we observed little difference between the limits w/wo interference1495
as shown in Figure I.2. Hence, we concluded that, in the region of large coupling where the analysis has1496
sufficient sensitivity, the interference effect is small enough.1497

J Impact of𝑾+heavy flavor fraction uncertainty1498
A normalization discrepancy of 10-30% between data and simulation by requiring heavy-flavor tags is1499
reported by Physics Modeling Group (TWiki).1500
Since we require a single𝑏-jet in the SR and vetoing the𝑏-jets in WCR, the discrepancy between data1501
and simulation cannot be evaluated in the CR, which may lead to a maximum 30% uncertainty while1502
extrapolating the𝑊+jets normalization from CR to SR. Currently, we are taking the uncertainty as the1503
medium value 20%. Variations of 10/20/30% are tested according to the instructions. The 95% CL1504
limits while applying different uncertainties are shown in Figure J.1. The ranking plots are also shown in1505
Figure J.2. The HF modeling uncertainty comes to the leading NP when we set it to 30% using signal1506
𝑀LQ = 1.5TeV, 𝑔𝑈 = 1.5, 𝛽23
𝐿 = 0.6. Meanwhile, the expected significance values for the three variations1507
are 2.156, 2.105, and 2.034, respectively. As a result, the increase in𝑊+jets heavy-flavor modeling1508
uncertainty leads to a maximum 5% decrease in significance.1509
We also compared the fit configurations for HF uncertainties correlated and decorrelated between two1510
SRs. The sensitivities are almost the same, but the ranking of uncertainties is changed. At the lower mass1511
region, the uncertainty for the resonance SR gives higher impact to the sensitivity, while at the higher mass1512
region, both resonance and non-resonance uncertainties are important. The ranking of decorrelated HF1513
uncertainties is shown in Figure J.3. Given that the𝑊+heavy flavor/light flavor ratio can depend on𝑝T, we1514
will use decorrelated NPs as a nominal configuration.1515

L A choice of WCR1520
Thenormalizationfactorfor 𝑊+jetsisestimatedin 0𝜏0𝑏WCR,andextrapolatedtothe 1𝜏1𝑏SR.Following1521
the PMG recommendation, as discussed in Appendix J, the additional uncertainty on𝑊+heavy flavor/light1522
flavor ratio is considered, but the pre-fit value 20% is a bit arbitral. We checked the validity in0𝜏1𝑏 VRs,1523
and observed 2𝜎 mild excess, as discussed in Appendix G. In this appendix, we tested the different choice1524
of WCR.1525
At the unblinding approval, the following 3 options were suggested by LPX conveners:1526
1. Use the0𝜏0𝑏 CR in the fit, and test the validity in0𝜏1𝑏 VR (the current choice);1527
2. Use the0𝜏1𝑏 VR as the WCR, and omit the𝑊+HF/LF uncertainty from the fit;1528
3. Useboth 0𝜏0𝑏and 0𝜏1𝑏regionsastheWCRsinthefit,togiveaconstrainton 𝑊+HF/LFuncertainty.1529
The S+B fit is performed and the corresponding limit is calculated for each option. The signal samples1530
used in this test is𝑀LQ = 2.5TeV, 𝑔𝑈 = 2.5, 𝛽23
𝐿 = 1.0. The obtained normalization factors in the different1531
fit configurations are shown in the Figure L.1. For the option 2, since the number of data events in0𝜏1𝑏1532
region is extremely smaller than the current0𝜏0𝑏 WCR, the post-fit uncertainty on𝜆𝑊 is 7–8 times larger1533
than the option 1. The post-fit pull for𝜆 𝑁 𝑜𝑛𝑅𝑒𝑠
𝑊 is 1.91 ± 0.52. The summary plots and NP ranking plots1534
are shown in Figure L.2 and Figure L.3. For the option 3, about1𝜎 pull is observed for the𝑊+jets HF1535
modleing uncertainty, as expected, and the post-fit uncertainty is constrained to about 0.9.1536
The best-fit mu values are:1537
𝜇 = 1.0+0.44
−0.35 = 1.0+0.40
−0.27 (stat)+0.16
−0.20 (syst) (L.1)
for the option 1,1538
𝜇 = 1.0+0.48
−0.39 = 1.0+0.43
−0.29 (stat)+0.196
−0.220 (syst) (L.2)
for the option 2, and1539
𝜇 = 1.0+0.46
−0.37 = 1.0+0.41
−0.20 (stat)+0.20
−0.24 (syst) (L.3)
for the option 3.1540
The post-fit yields for 3 options are shown in the Tables L.1–L.3. Three options show the consistent results1541
between each other. The𝑊+jets yield in non-resonance SR is increased for the option 2 and 3, compared1542
to the option 1, due to the larger𝜆𝑊 and the pulled𝑊+HF uncertainty, respectively. The limit plots for1543
different options are also shown in Figure L.4.1544

M 1𝝉0𝒍1𝒃 VR study1545
Thissectiondiscusses theadditionalstudyofthe 1𝜏0ℓ0𝑏 VR.Asintroduced intheSection7, SRis defined1546
withthe 1𝜏0ℓ1𝑏 selections. TodefinetheVRwiththesame 1𝜏0ℓ1𝑏 selections,weneedtousethedifferent1547
kinematic cuts from the SR, for example with the looser𝑚𝑏𝜏 and/or 𝑚T, which leads to the additional1548
systematic uncertainty only on these VRs. We therefore avoided to use such VRs, and used0𝜏1ℓ1𝑏 region1549
to validate the0𝑏 → 1𝑏 extrapolation, where we found the discrepancy between data and simulation after1550
performing the background-only fit, as discussed in Appendix G. Although it seems that the discrepancy1551
mostly comes from the statistical fluctuation, it would be helpful to find an additional1𝑏 VR to verify this1552
conclusion. New VRs with the1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 selections are defined by reverting the invariant mass and𝑚T cut in1553
resonance and non-resonance SR, respectively:1554
𝑚𝑏𝜏 > 800 GeV → 400 < 𝑚 𝑏𝜏 < 800 GeV (M.1)
for the resonance1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 VR, and1555
𝑚T(𝜏, 𝐸miss
T ) > 600 GeV → 500 < 𝑚T(𝜏, 𝐸miss
T ) < 600 GeV (M.2)
for the non-resonance1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 VR. In this study, we do not consider any additional systematic uncertainties1556
on the𝑚𝑏𝜏 and 𝑚T modeling.1557
Additionally,the 𝑚Tand 𝑝 𝜏
T requirementsareloosenedby100 GeVintheresonantVR.Forthenon-resonant1558
VR, the𝐸miss
T threshold is lowered by 200GeVand the𝑝 𝜏
T requirement is loosened by 100GeVto increase1559
the statistical yield.1560
The signal contamination in these new1𝜏0𝑙1𝑏 VRs is checked before examining the data. For the coupling1561
parameters, we choose the same ones as those used in Sec. 7.3. The signal contamination is 2% in the1562
resonance VR and 7% in the non-resonance VR.1563
The normalization factors derived by the background-only fits to CRs are applied to these new VR. The1564
post-fit number of background yields is(25.4 ± 1.5) and (8.0 ± 1.7) for resonance and non-resonance VR,1565
respectively, and the observed data are 24 and 9. The numbers of data events in the lower mass regions of1566
SRs are well described by the current background estimation model.1567

N SR0b optimisation1568
During the ATLAS circulation, we found that the contamination of signals with large𝛽23
𝐿 in VRW-Res1569
and VRW-NonRes could be significant. Therefore we adjust the fit strategy and use WVR-Res and1570
WVR-NonRes as our new SR0b-Res and SR0b-NonRes in the fit since we can gain sensitivity from both1571
regions. Meanwhile, the discussion of the necessity of the optimisation for the SR0b is raised naturally.1572
Since we observed a large interference contribution in the current SR0b and many background events1573
remaining, we expect less interference effect and less SM background yields with considerable signal1574
yields after the cut optimisation, which finally leads to a better sensitivity.1575
For simplicity, we performed the optimisation with only a few leading systematics for both SR0b-Res and1576
SR0b-NonRes. The signal parameter (𝑀LQ, 𝑔𝑈, 𝛽23
𝐿 ) of (1.5TeV, 0.5, 1.8) and (3.0TeV, 2.0, 1.8) are used1577
for SR0b-Res and SR0b-NonRes optmisation, respectively. The𝐸miss
T cut is scanned from 200GeVto1578
1500GeVwith a scan step of 100GeVto find the cut that maximizes the significance. The significance1579
scan results are shown in the Figure N.1. The best points are 600GeVand 400GeVfor SR0b-Res and1580
SR0b-NonRes, respectively. For SR0b-NonRes, the current definition is the same as the optimized𝐸miss
T1581
cut. So there is no need to change the SR0b-NonRes definition. For SR0b-Res, as shown in Figure N.2, we1582
observed a maximum 30% improvement and 10% improvement at 1.5TeVand 2.0TeV, respectively. The1583
interference effect is also significantly suppressed due to the high𝐸miss
T cut as shown in Figure N.3. Since1584
the Resonance process contribution is much less than the non-resonance process at higher mass, only 2%1585
improvement is expected at 3.0 TeV.1586
Figure N.1: The significance scanned by𝐸miss
T for SR0b-Res and SR1b-NonRes, respectively. Only a few leading
systematics are considered.
Given that 1.5TeVis already excluded by pair-production searches, and considering the unblinding policy.1587
Although LQ masses larger than 1.8TeVhave not been excluded, the main scope of this analysis is to1588
quickly make results public using only Run-2 data and to explore previously unexplored phase space. In1589
